TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* What a New Rebel Alliance Means for Congo
  Foreign Policy | 22 Oct 00:36 UTC
  Former President Joseph Kabila has joined a coalition that seeks to challenge Kinshasa “by all means.”
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Africa Brief. The highlights this week: A new rebel alliance in eastern Congo threatens to drag the rest of the country into war, Mauritius experiences its worst riots in decades, and Egyptian President Abdel Fattah al-Sisi outlines a succession plan. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s Africa Brief. The highlights this week: A new rebel alliance in eastern Congo threatens to drag the rest of the country into war, Mauritius experiences its worst riots in decades, and Egyptian President Abdel Fattah al-Sisi outlines a succession plan. A new development in the decades-long conflict in the eastern Democratic Republic of the Congo threatens to draw the rest of the country into a wider war. Last week, former President Joseph Kabila, who led Congo from 2001 to 2019, joined an armed coalition that seeks to challenge the current administration “by all means, including force.” Now, some analysts believe that an insurgency that has so far been concentrated in eastern territories could make its way to the capital of Kinshasa in the country’s west. Kinshasa “now confronts a more formidable opposition: an armed movement with demonstrated military capabilities, led by a former president with extensive political networks,” commentator Samson Kasujja wrote last week in the Great Lakes Eye . The new coalition , called the Front for the Refoundation of the Nation (FRN), unites militias and opposition figures under one banner. It includes the Rwanda-backed M23 movement, which has controlled vast areas of eastern Congo since 2022, and its broader political coalition, the Congo River Alliance, led by Corneille Nangaa, the former head of the country’s electoral commission. The FRN’s members have diverse goals. M23, for instance, purports to seek protection—and a greater political voice—for Congo’s minority Tutsi population, which has long faced discrimination . Kabila, meanwhile, likely aims to use his political connections to unseat Congo’s current president, Félix Tshisekedi, his former coalition partner turned rival . But on a broader level, Congo’s conflict is rooted in competition over land and mineral resources. Congo produces more than 70 percent of the world’s cobalt and is home to deposits of other metals that are essential to the technology and defense industries. The escalation of the insurgency demonstrates the failure of a so-called peace deal between Rwanda and Congo brokered by the Trump administration last year, whereby the Congolese govern

Something else about Rubio’s visit to Mumbai could help the bilateral relationship. * The Paradox at the Heart of China’s Export Boom
  Foreign Policy | 21 Oct 21:08 UTC
  China’s domestic economic woes are helping fuel its success abroad.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: Chinese exports make their biggest monthly jump in 16 years, the Chinese Communist Party prepares for an important political meeting , and the former White House press secretary’s law career in China draws scrutiny. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. The highlights this week: Chinese exports make their biggest monthly jump in 16 years, the Chinese Communist Party prepares for an important political meeting , and the former White House press secretary’s law career in China draws scrutiny. China’s exports grew by an astonishing 36.3 percent year-over-year in September, their biggest monthly jump since July 2010 , thanks to the artificial intelligence industry and the easing of trade tensions with the United States. It’s only one month, but it fits a pattern of soaring exports since the COVID-19 pandemic that cemented China’s place as the world’s factory. Exports will likely exceed $4 trillion this year. Unlike the manufacturing boom of the 2000s, this one is increasingly concentrated in high-end sectors such as electric vehicles, smartphones, and power grid management. The paradox is that this success is partly thanks to a domestic economy still staggering from the twin blows of the pandemic and the ensuing real estate crisis. The latter has devastated household finances, while the former made people even more nervous about spending, especially given how little public support most households received during that time. Those weaknesses fueled the manufacturing surge in several ways. Deflation at home, especially at the factory gate , made Chinese products more price-competitive. But that may be coming to an end this year; producer prices have risen for seven consecutive months as the government pushes to rebalance supply and demand. Manufacturing has also benefited from cheaper credit as the property sector has crashed. Real estate lending once formed an unhealthy loop in which property giants borrowed from banks to buy land from local governments. That loop has broken , with land sales down 30 percent this year alone and loans flowing into manufacturing instead. A tight regulatory environment at home has also made foreign markets more tempting for Chinese firms. Other factors are at work, too, including the growing demand for AI-related products and services and China’s heavy investment in alternative energy and EVs. But domestic weakness is what allows such astonishing export growth

Yet taken together, these rumors inadvertently reveal something fundamental about the workings of Chinese elite politics: Xi relies heavily on Li and Cai, his two most important lieutenants, to sustain his rule. Understanding Chinese elite politics requires identifying the relationships that determine how power operates, rather than obsessing over supposed clues in every meeting or photograph. One reason outside observers have repeatedly misjudged Xi’s rule is that they have become lost in the fog of power struggles, imagining Zhongnanhai, the Chinese leadership compound, as a place perpetually on the verge of a palace coup while overlooking how the system functions on a daily basis. However much power Xi has concentrated in his own hands, he cannot personally manage the enormous machinery of the Chinese state, or the vast CCP itself. He needs lieutenants. * NATO 3.0 Is a Dangerous Gamble
  Foreign Policy | 21 Oct 16:11 UTC

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Last week, NATO began its annual Steadfast Noon exercise to ensure the alliance’s nuclear readiness, even as the Trump administration pursues a policy that threatens to undermine it. Launched in February, NATO 3.0 is the brainchild of Elbridge Colby, the undersecretary of defense for policy. At its core is the assumption that the United States can rapidly wind down its conventional commitments to Europe while retaining its leading role in the alliance, so long as Europe continues to rely on the U.S. nuclear guarantee. Colby presents this shift as a rebalancing of the alliance, enticing European states to take responsibility for the continent’s conventional defense as Washington turns its attention to the Indo-Pacific. Last week, NATO began its annual Steadfast Noon exercise to ensure the alliance’s nuclear readiness, even as the Trump administration pursues a policy that threatens to undermine it. Launched in February, NATO 3.0 is the brainchild of Elbridge Colby, the undersecretary of defense for policy. At its core is the assumption that the United States can rapidly wind down its conventional commitments to Europe while retaining its leading role in the alliance, so long as Europe continues to rely on the U.S. nuclear guarantee. Colby presents this shift as a rebalancing of the alliance, enticing European states to take responsibility for the continent’s conventional defense as Washington turns its attention to the Indo-Pacific. The approach is Washington’s attempt to have it both ways—to cut back without sacrificing its influence or affecting extended nuclear deterrence. In reality, however, it would compromise Europe’s defense, diminish Washington’s sway, weaken the U.S. nuclear guarantee, and generate proliferation risks—ultimately undercutting the policy’s objectives and U.S. national interests. Colby’s vision is already reshaping the U.S. footprint in Europe. In May, the Department of Defense announced plans to withdraw roughly 5,000 troops from Germany and cancel the deployment of a long-range strike battalion there. The U.S. rotational brigade in Romania returned home without replacement last October, and reports suggest another 25,000 to 40,000 troops could leave Europe. Washington has also proposed cutting its contribution to the NATO Force Model—the pool of forces made available for NATO operations—by one-third to one-half . * The West Has a Crisis of Hope
  Foreign Policy | 21 Oct 14:41 UTC
  A former president of Estonia on the growing tide of global protest—and how today’s leaders can win back ordinary people.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. As young people take to the streets in countries across Europe, it is important to bluntly name the crisis that afflicts rich Western societies: hopelessness. Every nation has its own unique problems, and particular issues such as immigration or inflation can spark a broader protest, but it is becoming clear that growing numbers of people feel society is not organized to serve their interests. What does an average worker want? To serve in their profession with dignity and earn a salary that allows for independent life decisions. Through their hard-earned money, they hope to afford the possibility of ascending the property ladder, paying for their children’s education, and having reasonable access to healthcare. This is the crux of what we in Europe call a social market economy. And this is what people expect when they do their part, which is to study, work, and pay taxes. As young people take to the streets in countries across Europe, it is important to bluntly name the crisis that afflicts rich Western societies: hopelessness. Every nation has its own unique problems, and particular issues such as immigration or inflation can spark a broader protest, but it is becoming clear that growing numbers of people feel society is not organized to serve their interests. What does an average worker want? To serve in their profession with dignity and earn a salary that allows for independent life decisions. Through their hard-earned money, they hope to afford the possibility of ascending the property ladder, paying for their children’s education, and having reasonable access to healthcare. This is the crux of what we in Europe call a social market economy. And this is what people expect when they do their part, which is to study, work, and pay taxes. This social contract has been eroding in the developed West. The last several decades of growth in industrial productivity are reversing and the influence of trade unions declining. To understand this, we need to look back to the 1960s and ’70s, as post-World War II societies saw the proportion of total economic output paid to workers in the form of salaries and benefits grow. That share of labor to GDP, as it is known, peaked in countries that are part of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) in the 1970s at around 70 percent. For most of these countries, the share of labor to GDP dropped in the 2000s to between 50 and 60 percent. Put simply, ordinary
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/21/gen-z-protests-youth-students-europe-france-milan-living-costs/

JERUSALEM—For 75 days starting in 2020, Luwam Habteab slept on the floor of a prison cell, given little food and no medical care. She was detained that November by Ethiopian security forces for her reporting on the Axum massacre, in which Eritrean forces killed hundreds of civilians over a weekend—one of the deadliest episodes of the 2020-22 Tigray war. Upon her release, Habteab found that her apartment had been sealed by federal police. Her family had warned her not to return there, telling her that security forces were waiting. * Trump Is Making a Mess of Diego Garcia
  Foreign Policy | 21 Oct 12:29 UTC
  Trying to reverse the Chagos Islands treaty only plays into China’s hands.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Rarely does such a tiny island cause such a fuss, but this has certainly been the case with Diego Garcia. Situated in the central Indian Ocean as part of the Chagos archipelago, Diego Garcia is a British-administered atoll claimed by Mauritius. It hosts a sprawling U.S.-British military base with key naval, air, space, communications, and logistics facilities. Throughout much of the Cold War, Diego Garcia served as a critical node to counter Soviet movements across the Indian Ocean and as an enabler to conduct operations in South Asia, Africa, the Middle East, and beyond. After nearly two years of negotiations, London and Port Louis agreed in 2024 to transfer sovereignty over Diego Garcia and the rest of the Chagos archipelago to Mauritius, and they formalized the arrangement in May 2025. (The Chagossians remain divided over their homeland’s political future and were largely excluded from the negotiations.) Under the treaty, British and American forces would retain access to their base on Diego Garcia under a 99-year lease from Mauritius, with an option to extend the arrangement for another 40 years. Flávio Bolsonaro—the eldest son of former President Jair Bolsonaro—and President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva to a runoff. Another key race was decided that day: In congressional elections, right-wing parties triumphed and are expected to hold at least 45 of 81 seats in the Senate. Brazil’s Senate confirms (and can remove) justices on the Supreme Court, whose expanding judicial reach has made it a central arbiter of Brazilian democracy. Decisions by the bench could yet shape the outcome of the runoff, set for Oct. 25. On Sept. 15, Chief Justice Edson Fachin convened a televised session to decide whether Justice Alexandre de Moraes—who oversaw Jair Bolsonaro’s prosecution and is reviled by the far right—should be investigated by the Federal Police. (Brazilian law requires courts to authorize investigations into their own members.) The justices never reached a decision: They split on a procedural motion, with Moraes voting on his own case, before Justice Flávio Dino asked for up to 90 days to study the file. That clock runs until mid-December—well after the runoff. The case swirling around Moraes stems from the collapse last year of Banco Master , a midsize Brazilian lender. The scandal—like the Operation Car Wash investigation of the mid-2010s—has ensnared many members of Brazil’s political and business elite. Federal Police hope to examine whether Moraes improperly assisted Master’s controlling shareholder, Daniel Vorcaro, and whether that conduct was connected to a legal services contract between Master and Moraes’s wife’s law firm. Moraes denies wrongdoing. He has not been charged with a crime. The scandal has become a hot topic on the campaign trail, and one likely to benefit Flávio. By putting off a decision
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/21/brazil-election-lula-bolsonaro-banco-master-supreme-court-moraes/

* Revealed: Expensive gas behind ‘nearly 90%’ of rise in UK electricity bills
  Carbon Brief | 21 Oct 11:37 UTC
  A surge in wholesale costs driven by expensive gas is responsible for nearly 90% of the rise in UK household… The post Revealed: Expensive gas behind ‘nearly 90%’ of rise in UK electricity bills appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: A surge in wholesale costs driven by expensive gas is responsible for nearly 90% of the rise in UK household electricity bills since 2020, reveals new analysis. The figures, shared with Carbon Brief by thinktank Nesta , show that wholesale gas costs are also behind the vast majority (77%) of the increase in household gas bills. The analysis firmly contradicts the idea, which has become embedded in UK media and political narratives , that the UK’s net-zero target is to blame for high energy bills. Andrew Sissons , director for sustainable future at Nesta, tells Carbon Brief that net-zero is, in fact, “helping to shelter Britain from the impact of expensive gas”. He adds: “Rising gas prices, not net-zero, have driven most of the recent surge in Britain’s energy bills. Britain is particularly exposed, with over 80% of households connected to gas mains, while our electricity system still relies too much on gas as the marginal unit.” The analysis comes after the UK government gave its approval for the Jackdaw gas field to be developed in the North Sea, despite stating that it “would not…affect prices”. In January 2026, Carbon Brief reported that two-thirds of the rise in electricity bills since before the 2022 global energy crisis was due to high gas prices. Now, amid ongoing conflict in the Middle East, wholesale gas prices are at their highest levels since Russia’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine . Indeed, as of 19 October, UK wholesale gas prices stood at 220p per therm (£75 per megawatt hour, MWh), above the average for 2022 of 210p/therm (£72/MWh). The figure below shows that, after accounting for inflation, typical UK annual household electricity bills had risen £180 by the fourth quarter of 2026, relative to the same period in 2020. Some 90% of this increase (£161) is due to wholesale costs, driven by the price of gas. In contrast – and contrary to widespread perceptions – renewable “ contracts for difference ” (CfDs) are reducing electricity bills by £17 under the current price cap, according to the Nesta analysis. In addition, the government has cut policy costs for electricity by moving 75% of legacy renewable support onto general taxation, ending the “ECO” efficiency scheme and temporarily removing VAT . Network costs have added £72 to typical bills since 2020, according to Nesta. Most of this (£46) is for investment in the electricity transmission network, known as the “ motorways ” of the grid. Only £21 of the rise in network costs relates to balancing th
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/revealed-expensive-gas-behind-nearly-90-of-rise-in-uk-electricity-bills

Newsrooms across the political spectrum fed “It Was Either That or Die” into other AI detection sites, with conflicting results. For Pangram’s false positive rates, it should be noted, are not negligible. The company estimates that its overall false positive rate is 1 in 10,000, though other factors, including the language of the text, can affect that rate. * The Longreads Questionnaire, Featuring Jasper Craven
  Longreads | 20 Oct 10:00 UTC
  He wrote the book on how the military shapes masculine identity. He'll also tell you about Disney adults, Pharaoh Sanders, and his favorite spot for a burger and negroni.
  FULL TEXT: Earlier this year, during an appearance on Kareem Rahma’s popular Subway Takes show, Jasper Craven explained line-crossing ceremonies, in which sailors celebrate first crossings of the equator. In US naval history, line-crossing ceremonies have at times involved violent hazing. But they have also involved pageantry, ritualistic performances of masculinity and femininity that tip over into drag. “Young men want love, support, community,” Craven said. “The military provides a permission structure to show love, often in very pure ways.” It’s a characteristic insight from Craven, a freelance reporter who has made a singular beat from the intersection of masculinity and the American military. In his magazine features, Craven has scrutinized Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s opposition to women in the armed forces via Hegseth’s undergraduate fits over The Vagina Monologues , and profiled Bernarr McFadden , a bodybuilder and publisher who oversaw two military schools “to impose his love of physical strength onto American children, the War Department, and the world.” Craven’s recent book, God Forgives, Brothers Don’t , follows some of our most insidious models of masculinity back to the military education programs that have shaped them for centuries. Craven is a dynamic counterexample to the men he writes about, less interested in bulking up the boundaries of his identity than testing their limits. For a memorable Harper’s feature, he spent six months undercover as an employee of the world’s largest private-security firm. (During his first shift, a supervisor criticized Craven for his non-detachable necktie, which could be used to strangle him.) He is at once stridently critical and deeply compassionate. (He is also, as you’ll learn, deeply and truly funny.) “There are myriad other ways to prove your manhood, and they’re not simply being emotionally vulnerable or non-violent,” Craven told an interviewer earlier this year. “You can actually fight for things: fight for peace, be a little risky, be a little reckless, break the law, put your body on the line. * How intuitions work
  Aeon | 20 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Just as seeing a tree gives you reason to believe it’s there, sensing that a stranger is creepy gives you reason to walk away - by Marc A Moffett Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: At the Campidoglio, Rome, 1984. Photo by Richard Kalvar/Magnum When people say such things, they are not typically drawing conclusions from a chain of reasoning. The person who avoids getting in the stranger’s car is not thinking: ‘He displayed these three behavioural cues and, therefore, he poses a risk.’ The email isn’t flagged by going through an explicit checklist. The deal doesn’t announce its flaws through a formal audit. In each case, something is simply presented as having a certain character: the man as creepy, the email as fraudulent, the deal as suspect. And yet, acting on these intuitions seems not merely excusable but entirely reasonable . If the man really does strike you as creepy, staying out of his car was probably the smart call – even if you can’t say why. Despite this, ‘vibes’ and ‘intuitions’ are often dismissed both in daily life and in academic enquiry as irrational or merely heuristic devices. And while we cannot always trust such reactions, they are central, indeed indispensable, to our ability to rationally navigate the world. Philosophers, however, have done surprisingly little to illuminate the rational credentials of these ordinary kinds of intuitions. Instead, they have focused the bulk of their attention on a much more rarefied kind of intuition called rational intuition , which is taken to function primarily in the a priori disciplines of philosophy, logic and mathematics. My objective is to explain why our everyday intuitive reactions can have such rational force. The key is to see that intuition works in much the same way as perception, in that both can directly present something to us as actual. Once we understand what gives perception this feature, we can see why intuitions might involve exercising the same basic kind of cognitive capacity. A standard way of characterising rational intuition is in terms of seemings , by analogy with perception. Just as it perceptually seems to you that there is a car in the garage, so it can intellectually seem to you that 2 + 2 = 4 – the former presenting a physical fact, the latter a conceptual one. But rational intuition and the everyday intuition at work when you decline to get in a stranger’s car are not as far apart as this rarefied example suggests: the philosopher’s intuition is, at bottom, a highly specialised version of the same basic capacity. To draw these two capacities together, let’s consider a favourite pastime of philosophers: the thought experiment. Philosophers have a

* Horrible, unmanly, indispensable

  Renaissance Italians saw the gun as the Devil’s work. But soon they embraced this unchivalrous – and necessary – weapon - by Catherine Fletcher Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Detail from The Battle of Pavia, 1525 ( c 1529) by Robert Heller. Courtesy the Nationalmuseum, Sweden /Wikipedia If you were asked to think of a gun owner, then Lorenzo de’ Medici – the celebrated patron of Renaissance art – would probably not be the first person to come to mind. In fact, however, Lorenzo ‘the Magnificent’ owned five portable firearms, which are listed in his 1492 postmortem inventory. Guns and gunpowder were controversial. In art and literature, they were labelled the Devil’s work, and were perceived as fundamentally unchivalrous. Yet within half a century, by the early months of 1539, Lorenzo’s descendant Duke Cosimo de’ Medici had 91 firearms in his Florentine storerooms. Somehow, in the courts of late-Renaissance Italy, the gun had become an acceptable, even valued, weapon. Beyond the palazzi of the peninsula’s towns and cities, guns were also making inroads into more ordinary lives. There were no standing armies in the 16th-century Italian states. Their wars were fought through temporary mobilisations, and with considerable help from companies of mercenaries. Yet as the 1506 provision that established the Florentine militia made clear, ‘little hope’ could be placed ‘in foreign and hired arms’. Far better to establish a new magistracy with ‘full authority and power’ to enlist men from Florentine territory, at least 10,000 of them. One in 10 of these men were to be equipped with firearms, and 500 handguns ( schioppetti ) should be kept in the Palazzo della Signoria (the city hall, now known as the Palazzo Vecchio) along with ‘such money as should be necessary for the schioppetti ’, presumably to purchase gunpowder and shot. Three or four companies ( bandiere ) entirely of schioppettieri might also be established. This militia, most often noted for the role of Niccolò Machiavelli in its foundation, was to be organised after the fashion of the Germans, in whose cities there was a centuries-old tradition of civic defence and a duty on every male citizen to maintain suitable arms for the purpose. In fact, one of the Florentine militia’s early constables was German (a Giovanni Tedesco); another (Giliberto Spagnolo) came from Spain, a country making the running in the use of small arms on the battlefield. Giovanni Tedesco arranged target practice for his company, which from 1508 was extended more generally among the militia, with exercises taking place on a monthly basis. M ilitias were not new but, by the 1490s, large-scale foreign military

* How to be a (Roman) man
  Aeon | 16 Oct 10:00 UTC [also carried by: Longreads]
  Cicero’s ideal of manly virtue stressed the performance of self-control and bravery, not boastful egotism and domination - by Catharine Edwards Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Roman bust, mid-1st century CE. Courtesy the Met Museum , New York What does it mean to be a man? In the early 21st century, masculinity is contested and fraught with anxiety; influencers offer models of manliness involving fast cars, sexual conquests, and building up muscle. Before the rise of feminism, they suggest, in some supposed Golden Age, everyone had a shared sense of what it meant to be a man – and being a man was unproblematically good. The Roman Empire, in their view, offers men today an inspirational example of power, hierarchy and achievement, which is why men think about it so much – unlike women, who are too immersed in their feelings… Manliness certainly was celebrated in the works of Cicero, republican Rome’s most eloquent statesman. In the mid-1st century BCE, his persuasive powers in the law courts and the senate were second to none; his speeches went on to inspire would-be orators well into the 20th century. He is often seen as the very embodiment of the Roman republic. The political world in which he flourished was a self-consciously masculine one; to be accused of acting like a woman was, for a Roman man, a grievous insult. Yet in Cicero’s writing we can find other strains too. For Roman men, anything labelled feminine was necessarily to be avoided, something to be ashamed of. But what exactly did it mean to act like a man? Ancient Rome in the 2nd and 1st centuries BCE saw the rise of a succession of military leaders who were promoted as masculine role models. The great general Marius won a series of victories in Spain, North Africa and on Italy’s northern frontier. For several years in a row, he was elected consul, one of Rome’s two chief executive officers. Unlike many other Roman politicians, he didn’t have an aristocratic background. One Roman writer imagines Marius fending off accusations that he was coarse in his manners: Here was a model of manliness forged on the battlefield. Given the military might of ancient Rome, it is surely unsurprising that Romans often associated masculinity with courage in battle. The word virtus , a term cognate with the Roman vir (meaning man as opposed to woman), clearly means ‘courage in war’ when it is first recorded in the mid-5th century BCE. Courage in battle could be taken as the foremost manifestation of Roman masculinity.   https://aeon.co/essays/the-politics-of-masculinity-at-the-end-of-the-roman-republic

  Rest of World | 16 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Chinese open-weight AI models offer high capability at significantly lower costs than U.S. frontier systems, but face monetization and compute challenges, experts said at a Rest of World event in San Francisco.
  FULL TEXT: A slew of new open-weight artificial intelligence models from American and European companies aim to convert users of Chinese open models who are concerned about security, but fall short of the dominant Chinese systems on performance despite having greater resources, experts said at a Rest of World event in San Francisco. Last week, American startup Reflection launched its first open-weight model , and European labs Aleph Alpha and Mistral also released new open-weight models. Open models such as these offer users greater customization and control than the closed models of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google at significantly lower costs. For businesses and governments in lower-income countries, in particular, cost is the primary factor, as well as the expertise needed to customize open models for their specific needs , including in areas such as education, healthcare, and farming, Daniel Björkegren, an assistant professor at Columbia University, said at the Rest of World event last week. But “one enduring question is, how easy is it to switch?” Since the launch of DeepSeek early last year, Chinese open models have rapidly closed in on their U.S. rivals. The post If Taiz Falls, Yemen May Never Be the Same appeared first on New Lines Magazine ...
  FULL TEXT: Taiz governorate in southwestern Yemen was never simply another front in the country’s war. The seven-day lightning offensive in September — in which the Houthis seized the strategic port city of Mokha and the Bab al-Mandab Strait between the Red Sea and the Gulf of Aden — amounted to more than a change on a map. And several districts in the south, west and east of Taiz governorate collapsed between Sept. 13 and Oct. 10, extending Houthi control to additional strategic areas and consolidating their gains on Yemen’s western coast. Taiz governorate has been one of the most important centers of resistance to the Houthis throughout the conflict. Its demographic, political and historical weight means that battles there have an importance that extends far beyond the governorate itself. If the Houthis consolidate their hold on what remains of the countryside and the city of Taiz itself, they could reshape the balance of power in Yemen. Recent events reveal a painful paradox: Taiz was a popular base of support for the republican system, which was born in 1962 following the overthrow of the theocratic monarchy that ruled the country. The population also had combat experience. But locals failed to forge a unified military institution capable of protecting the area and honoring its sacrifices. The recent collapse was not simply the result of a shortage of fighters or a lack of will to fight; it was also the product of failures in command, coordination and supply, and the subordination of nationwide institution-building to partisan and personal interests. For roughly 12 years, Taiz governorate remained a major center of resistance, even while the Houthis controlled parts of the city of Taiz and large stretches of its countryside, including al-Hawban, the governorate’s most important economic and commercial center. But the endurance of its people and fighters never translated into a sustainable defense system. The front lines remained exposed to fragmented decision-making, competing interests and weak coordination, until the nightmare of a renewed siege returned. The question today is not only whether the Houthis will be able to take the city of Taiz, but what this development would mean for Yemen’s future. Would it represent a military setback that can be contained, or a strategic shift that could redraw the balance of power in western Yemen, weaken the anti-Houthi camp and deepen the divide between north and south? The Hyrtl Skull Collection is the first exhibit you notice when you walk into the Mütter Museum at the College of Physicians of Philadelphia. This is by curatorial design: The skulls face the main entrance, staring steadily at each person who crosses the threshold. All 139 are evenly spaced in long lines behind the glass doors of a towering cherrywood cabinet. Each one rests in a wire frame on top of a thin balsa box with a tag at its center. I recall tracking them with glances as I shuffled nearby with two friends, as part of a large crowd. Small bright lights illuminated them from inside the cases, making them glow dully from my angle. Seeing them for the first time, I was both mesmerized by this effect and intimidated by the skulls’ great number. I tried not to look for too long, but the density of people on the narrow walkway made that impossible: I was soon stuck in the dead center of the exhibit. Almost in spite of myself, I scanned the wall text and learned how they had been brought together to disprove a piece of 19th-century race science, which seemed like one of the better reasons to collect skulls. Most of the exhibits in the Mütter Museum have similarly odd origins. Tucked into a corner of the College of Physicians’ stately New Beaux-Arts building, the Mütter is a 19th-century medical teaching collection/cabinet museum with an annual visitorship of almost 150,000 people. Its staff goes on late-night talk shows with specimens in tow, hosts YouTube series. The gift shop sells soaps and keychains in the shapes of its artifacts. Coming out of a death-obsessed past into an incongruous living present, the exhibits are caught somewhere between scientific exemplar and exploitative spectacle. The museum acknowledges its reputation as a freak show and, in response, doubles down on the meanings of curiosity. Signs we saw at the main gate declared the place’s ethos: disturbingly informative . The text beside the skulls explained itself: Brought together is a euphemistic way of saying, assembled by grave robbing . Dr. Hyrtl dug up a lot of his collection, or had assistants do it for him. Or paid other collectors, other doctors. The graves were attached to hospitals, madhouses, prisons. Creative industries in the majority world have spent decades navigating piracy and weak enforcement, forcing them to think beyond rights-based policing about how creators actually receive value. It has approached AI pragmatically, using it to curate, tag, and attribute creative work rather than simply blocking and banning. In the West, fear of AI has helped turn pessimism into a cultural virtue. Would you publicly say you love AI? Your answer may say something about where you’re from. While the U.S. is building and deploying AI at a scale no other country matches, Americans are deeply anxious about where it is heading. Only 17% of U.S. adults think AI will have a positive impact on the country over the next 20 years, according to a 2025 Pew Research Center survey . In much of the Global South, the mood is markedly different . Stanford’s 2025 AI Index found that 83% of respondents in China, 80% in Indonesia, and 77% in Thailand said AI-powered products offer more benefits than drawbacks, compared with 39% in the U.S. and 36% in the Netherlands. Demographics are a part of the answer. Nearly 90% of the world’s young people live outside the West. They are not simply using AI, they are playing with it. * The ladder of knowledge
  Aeon | 15 Oct 10:00 UTC
  What we claim as ‘true’ is not a ceiling we are forever failing to reach but something that changes as we climb toward it - by Alexander V Gheorghiu Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Hamburg, Germany, 1937. Photo by Herbert List/Magnum Photos On the evening of 8 September 1930, the German mathematician David Hilbert stepped up to a microphone in Königsberg and addressed his countrymen by radio. He was 68, recently retired, and at the height of his fame. The talk was a defence of pure reason, delivered in the city of Immanuel Kant, and it ended with two phrases that would later be carved on his tombstone: Wir müssen wissen. Wir werden wissen . We must know. We shall know. David Hilbert. Courtesy the University of Göttingen Are certain questions closed to the human mind? Are there truths about the world that we can never know? Hilbert thought that, at least within mathematics, this could not be the case. For him, mathematics was a game played with symbols: one could fix the right axioms as rules of the game and every mathematical problem could be solved. He was wrong. The day before his Königsberg address, at a small academic meeting in the same city, a 24-year-old Austrian named Kurt Gödel had quietly announced that no such system for arithmetic could exist. Whatever reasonable set of rules you fix, something true must lie beyond them and thus beyond our knowledge. We can’t know. We won’t know. Gödel’s results, known as the Incompleteness Theorems, are landmark achievements of 20th-century logic. Recent work in philosophy and mathematics has begun to illuminate the gap they opened, and with it something deeper about the foundations of knowledge itself. The story begins in the same city, but two centuries earlier. W hen Kant remembered a book by David Hume he’d read years earlier, he was woken from what he called his ‘dogmatic slumber’. Kant had believed that pure reasoning could teach us every absolute truth about the world, but Hume had asked a question that that stumped him: what reason do we actually have to believe that the future will resemble the past? We may have seen the Sun rise a thousand times, but how do we know it will rise tomorrow? The belief that the future will resemble the past is, Hume argued, merely a kind of habit. Kant couldn’t answer Hume’s challenge and was silenced for a decade. For Hume, proving a theorem was like unpacking a suitcase to find an outfit. Kant could not leave it there When Kant finally broke his silence with the Critique of Pure Reason (1781), he offered a radical answer. We can know the world only as it appears to our minds, and that is where our science sits; the noumenal world – the Ding an si
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-century-long-search-for-the-limits-to-knowledge
